11-tab-kyverno.png	Include Pod, ReplicaSet, Job
11-tab-kyverno.png	Results on Pod, ReplicaSet, Job are usually a controller's copies of one finding - a Deployment's failure repeats on every ReplicaSet and Pod under it. Kyverno's CLI collapses those by rule
11-tab-kyverno.png	name, which the CEL family has none of, so they are filtered here, visibly: the switch shows them. Every count above includes them.
11-tab-kyverno.png	No failing, erroring or warning result on a resource the switch does not hide.
11-tab-kyverno.png	| Changes • O
11-tab-kyverno.png	A report is owned by its resource and dies with it, so Kyverno keeps no history. The dashboard records a problem that appeared or cleared between two reads - a cleared one is either fixed
11-tab-kyverno.png	or gone with its resource - for as long as the deployment's retention keeps them (kyverno. eventsRetentionDays).
11-tab-kyverno.png	No change recorded yet: the first read of a cluster is a baseline, not a burst of appearances.

12-tab-namespace-audit.png	High
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	2
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	I Why this is a risk
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Offboarding does not revoke it
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Removing someone from an LDAP group revokes their
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	access everywhere at once. A binding that names the
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	person keeps granting after they leave the team, change
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	role, or leave the company - until somebody remembers
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	it exists.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Invisible to access review
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Every group-based review - including the rest of this
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	dashboard - answers "who is in the group?". These
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	grants are attached to no group, so a clean review can
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	coexist with standing access nobody approved.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	No approval trail
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Group membership is granted in the enterprise directory,
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	with a request and an approver. A hand-made binding
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	records only who ran oc, and only for as long as the audit
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	log is retained.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	| Cluster-wide direct grants • 2
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	These name a person at the cluster scope, so each one grants every namespace on this cluster - all 128 of them. A scope, not a namespace: the table below ranks namespaces only, and the
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Namespaces at risk tile above counts it as one entry.

12-tab-namespace-audit.png	• Show the 2
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	I How to close it
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Work top-down: the table above is already in the order to do it in.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	1. Identify the role. Match the grant to the LDAP group the operator already syncs for that privilege - the naming convention encodes it (<app>-ns-admin, -ns-developer, -
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	ns-audit).
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	2. Add the person to that group in the enterprise directory, through the normal request-and-approve path. Access now has an owner and an audit trail.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	3. Wait one sync and confirm on the Groups tab that they appear as a member. Do not skip this - deleting first leaves them locked out.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	4. Delete the direct binding. oc delete rolebinding ‹name> -n ‹namespace>
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	5. Confirm it is gone here on the next refresh.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Operational goal: zero rows on this page. At zero, one directory change offboards a person from the entire cluster, and an access review that reads clean is clean.
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	Every grant
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	• Show 12 grants
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	The per-namespace table above is the view to work from. This is the flat detail list - collapsed by default on purpose, because on a cluster with thousands of direct grants rendering every
12-tab-namespace-audit.png	row costs time nobody gets value from.

14-tab-usage.png	Times render in the server's timezone; the Day column is a UTC date. That split is deliberate rather than an oversight: the day is a stored bucket, computed server-side when the activity was
14-tab-usage.png	recorded, so re-labelling it local would misstate rows already written, and re-bucketing would make the same column mean one thing before a deploy and another after. Near midnight a
14-tab-usage.png	session can therefore sit on the following UTC day.
14-tab-usage.png	An interaction is one deliberate action - opening the dashboard, switching tab, drilling in, changing a filter - not one HTTP request: the page refreshes itself every 60s and counting those
14-tab-usage.png	measured how long a tab was left open rather than whether anyone used it. Reading time is invisible, so a long look at one page counts once.
14-tab-usage.png	These are not logins either: the proxy owns this dashboard's session, so nothing here records a sign-in to it. Logins to the cluster are a separate record with a separate source - the auth-
14-tab-usage.png	server's own log - and they are on the Logins tab.
